The order of the answer: while the text is empty, `orca_remote_branches` puts first the remote branches that were picked before in this list (the pick of a remote row of A-25, and of the ref and of the existing branch of A-9) and that Orca's answer holds, the newest pick first, and the others follow in the order Orca returns them (a remembered branch that the first 50 of Orca's answer lack is asked of Orca by its name, one question for each, at most 5, and is added only when Orca answers a branch of exactly that name, so a branch that is gone is not added; a failed question adds nothing and does not fail the list), so the five shown are the last five picked, filled up with Orca's own first; a text leaves the answer as Orca gave it. The sorting is a pure function of Rust (`recent_first`), and the page only shows the first five rows it gets. No time is shown, because Orca gives none for a remote branch (the answer of `repo.searchRefs` is names only). The owner chose this order (2026-11-15, issue #653).
- A section with no row to show says 「該当するブランチがありません」 once its answer has come, and a read that failed shows its message in its section (in the warning color) and does not empty the other.

The switch. A pick closes the list and calls `orca_switch_branch(path, kind, name, environment)` with `kind` `local` for a local row and `remote` for a remote row (Orca makes the local branch that tracks it). A new branch cannot be made from here. When it answers, the tree of the pane is read again from the root with the folders that are open (the read of A-18's return of the focus) and the list of the screen is read again (`refreshOrca`), so the heading shows the new branch (on a saved environment, whose list is streamed, Rust has the stream read `worktree.ps` again, A-12; issue #600); the tabs of the work pane stay open, and the files and differences that the panes show are read again as by the clock of A-18 (a file with unsaved typing is left as it is, and is refused at its next save by A-18's own check when its text differs from the disk). A switch that worked with a `warning` (a step after the switch failed) shows 「{branch} に切り替えました: {warning}」 in a notice. A refusal (a change not committed, a merge or rebase in progress, an agent at work, and so on) shows Orca's own words on a card (A-28), as the other errors of the pane do, and nothing is read again. When the pane comes to show another worktree, an open list is closed.

What is remembered. A pick of a remote row (and a pick of the marked remote row again, where the list of A-9 allows it) calls `orca_branch_used(repo, name, environment)` with the key of the project (`OrcaProject.key`), the name as Orca names it and the Orca of the list (`null` is this machine); a local row calls nothing. Rust keeps, for each pair of an Orca and a repository, the last 5 names picked, the newest first, and a name picked again moves to the front; it keeps at most 50 pairs, and drops the pair picked from longest ago, so the file does not grow without end. It is saved in `orca-branch-recent.json` in the app config directory (`{"pairs": [{"environment", "repo", "names"}]}`, the pair picked last first), through a temporary file and a rename (`write_atomic`), by a call that reads the file again and writes all of it. A file that is missing, cannot be read or is not that shape remembers nothing, and the next pick writes it again. Rust refuses a `repo` that `orca_remote_branches` would refuse, a blank `name`, one with a control character or over 512 bytes, and an `environment` that is blank, has a control character or is over 256 bytes (a Japanese message); nothing is asked of Orca. The call only changes the order of the list, so a failure of it neither stops the pick (the switch, the ref or the existing branch) nor shows anything.

The calls. `orca_local_branches` and `orca_remote_branches` check the worktree against the target's list and refuse an SSH host's, as the file commands do (A-18); `orca_switch_branch` reads the list each time, as a save does, and forgets what it believed of the worktree afterwards. The names and the answers were read in the `app.asar` of Orca 1.4.256 and are not a public interface; `git.switchBranch` was never run on a real Orca, because it changes a worktree, and a saved environment was not tried. An Orca update can change them (`requirements/constraints.md`, Orca).

No screen text explains the list; the filter field and the list are named by their `aria-label` and headings only (`frontend.md`).

Unit tests (`cargo test`) cover the git branch of a worktree read from the list (`worktrees_of` in `orca.rs`: `refs/heads/` removed, none for a detached or missing one), the branches read, the remote search, the checks of the arguments and the answer of a switch (`orca_files.rs`), and the branches remembered: the order of the answer (the remembered first, the ones the answer lacks left out, no change with a text), the remembered branches outside the first answer (asked by name, added only on an exact name, at most five questions, a failed question adds nothing), what is kept (the front, five names, the limit of the pairs, a pair for each Orca and repository), the file (missing, broken, written and read back) and the checks of the arguments. The E2E spec `orca-files.e2e.ts` mocks the commands and covers the button and its place in the heading, the list under it with the two sections (their headings with the icon, the count and the sticky band, the first five remote branches and the button that opens and folds the rest, the filter that shows every match with no button and folds them again when it is cleared, and no button for five remote branches or fewer), the mark of the current branch, the faint and disabled branch in use, the filter on the local rows, the remote question (the arguments when it opens and after the typing), a pick of a local and of a remote branch (the arguments of the call, the tree and the list read again, the tabs kept), the remembering of a remote pick (the arguments of `orca_branch_used`, no call for a local row, and a failure of it that stops nothing and shows nothing; the same for the ref and the existing branch of A-9 in `orca-handoff.e2e.ts`), the heading of a saved environment after a switch from the app and after one made outside it (the list of the stream), a switch with a warning, a refusal on a card (A-28), a worktree whose Orca name differs from its git branch (the button shows the branch, before and after a switch), and no button for a worktree on no branch.

## A-26 Rich Markdown view

A Markdown file open in the editor of the Orca screen (A-18) opens in a rich view: its headings, lists, tables, code and so on drawn as a document, not as marks in a text area. The heading of the editor has two buttons for it, the source (`<>`) and the rich view (the pencil), and the file is shown in the one that is pressed. The rich view is edited there and saved as any typing is (below). Rust names the language (A-21), cuts the front matter off and puts an edit into the original text (`src-tauri/src/markdown_patch.rs`, the commands `markdown_split` and `markdown_patch`); the frontend is `ts/orca-editor.ts`, with `ts/vendor-load.ts`, `src/vendor/milkdown.js` and `src/style.css`.

Why. The owner asked for a rich editor for Markdown files in the Orca editor, in parts: the view (2026-11-12, issue #380, part #384), the putting of an edit into the original text (part #381, `markdown_patch`), and the editing and saving in the rich view (part #382). A button for the difference, a toolbar, formulas and diagrams are other parts and are not here.

When it applies. A file whose `language` in the answer of `orca_file_read` (A-21) is `markdown` (`.md`, `.mdx`, and the other names Orca's table gives to Markdown). A file of another language has neither button and is the text area of A-18, as it was. The two buttons (`.orca-edit-mode`, `aria-label` 「ソース」 and 「リッチ」, `aria-pressed` on the one whose view is shown) are at the right end of the heading, after the state of the save, and are drawn once the file is read.

The views. The file opens in the rich view. The rich view (`.orca-edit-rich`) takes the place of the box of line numbers and text area (`.orca-edit-body`, which is hidden then), and the source button shows that box again and gives the text area the focus. The choice is kept for the file, in memory only, while it is open (a tab shown again keeps it; closing the tab drops it), and is saved nowhere. The text that the rich view draws is the text of the text area at the moment it is shown, so typing made in the source is in the rich view after the switch, and an edit made in the rich view is in the text area before the source is shown (see "Editing"); the automatic save of A-18 goes on in either view and saves the text of the text area. The limit of A-18 (a file over 384 KiB is not opened) is as it was.

The rich view. Milkdown draws the body of the text in an editor (`contenteditable`; read only for a file that cannot be edited in it, see "Editing"): the CommonMark and GFM presets, so tables, task lists, strikethrough and links are drawn too. The front matter (a block between `---` lines at the start) is cut off by Rust (`markdown_split(text)` answers `front` and `body`) and only the body is given to Milkdown. An image whose address is not `data:` or of the app is not loaded (the CSP is not loosened, `frontend.md`). If Milkdown cannot be read or the cut fails, the source is shown and the message goes to a card (A-28).

The paragraph of the caret (issue #885). While the rich view is edited and has the focus and no range is selected, the paragraph the caret is in (a heading, a code block, a table cell, or a paragraph, also that of a list item) has the same faint band as the line of the caret in the source (A-18, `--cursor-band`), across the width of the editor and under its text (`.orca-edit-rich-band`, a box of the rich view outside the editor, so that the editor's own document is not touched); the unit is the paragraph, not the line, because the rich view wraps its lines. It follows the caret, and goes when a range is selected or the focus leaves the editor; the color of the text is not changed.

The library. Milkdown 7.23.0 (`@milkdown/kit`: core, the CommonMark and GFM presets, the history plugin and a small ProseMirror plugin of ours that tells the page that the document changed; MIT; 12,067 stars, last push 2026-11-12, read from the GitHub API on 2026-11-13), bundled with esbuild 0.28.2 (MIT; 40,141 stars, last push 2026-08-09, read the same way) into the one file `src/vendor/milkdown.js` by `scripts/build-milkdown.mjs` (`npm run vendor:milkdown`; the entry is `scripts/milkdown-entry.js`). Both are `devDependencies` and the app's own build is still `tsc` alone; the bundle is committed like the other files of `src/vendor/`, and holds the 87 packages Milkdown needs (ProseMirror, remark and so on, all MIT). It is a classic script that `ts/vendor-load.ts` reads the first time a Markdown file is drawn in the rich view, not at start-up (`#414`), under the global `TsunagiaiMilkdown`. It loads nothing from outside, uses no `eval` and no `new Function`, and its path is inside `src/vendor/`, so the CSP (`script-src 'self'`) is as it was. Milkdown has no theme here: `src/style.css` styles the elements of the rich view (headings, lists, task marks, quotes, code, tables) with the variables of `:root`.

Editing. The editor rewrites the notation of a text even when nothing is edited (bullet marks, the alignment of a table, indentation), so its export is never saved as it is. When the view is drawn, the page takes the export of the editor (`before`) and puts it through a second editor that is drawn nowhere (`TsunagiaiMilkdown.normalize`); the view can be edited only if that second export is the same text as `before` (loading and exporting twice is stable) and `markdown_patch(original, before, before)` answers `patched` with the original text (Rust answers `conflict` when the lines of the original cannot all be paired with the lines of the export, so a file it could not patch at an edit is known when it is opened). A file that fails either test is not edited in the rich view: it opens in the source (the first drawing of the file), and the rich view that the button shows afterwards is read only. After each change of the document the page waits 200 ms for more changes (at most one wait at a time), then sends `markdown_patch(original, before, after)`: `original` is the text of the text area when the view was drawn, `before` the export then, `after` the export now. Rust applies only the lines that differ between `before` and `after` to `original`, so every line the owner did not touch stays as it was in the file (its bullet mark, its spacing) and the front matter comes back as it was. An edit over several lines with a line only the original has between them (a comment the editor does not write out) is a `conflict`, not a change that drops that line. While the rich view is shown the text area's layers (colors, line numbers) are not drawn again at each edit; they are drawn when the source is shown. The answer `patched` replaces the text of the text area, which marks the file as changed and starts the wait of the automatic save, as typing in the source does; the heading shows the same states (changed mark, 「保存待ち」, 「保存中」, 「保存しました」, the failures of A-18). The automatic save, the close of the tab, the switch to the source and `pagehide` wait for an edit that is on its way to the text area, so no edit is lost. The answer `conflict` (or an error) leaves the text area as it was, switches the view to the source and sends the message to a card (A-28). The switch from the rich view to the source shows the text area with the edit in it, and the switch back draws the text of the text area, so the typing of either view is carried over.

The same bundle also draws and edits the Slack posting fields (S-21, the post to a channel, and S-6, the reply in a thread) and the body of a mail reply (M-10): `ts/rich-editor.ts` makes the editor with `TsunagiaiMilkdown.compose` and exports its Markdown, which is not patched into any original text and goes to Slack through `to_mrkdwn` (S-21) or into a mail as its Markdown and HTML parts (M-10). They load it the same way, the first time such a field is shown.

No new call to Orca, no new file saved and no new setting is added. No screen text explains the buttons (`frontend.md`).

Unit tests (`cargo test`) of the cut of the front matter and of putting an edit into the text are those of `markdown_patch.rs`. The E2E spec `orca-files.e2e.ts` mocks `markdown_split` and `markdown_patch` and covers a Markdown file drawn in the rich view (heading, bold, code, table, task items, editable, the source box hidden, the arguments of the cut and of the first `markdown_patch`), what is typed in the rich view reaching the arguments of the save through `markdown_patch` (`original` is the file's text with its front matter and its own notation, `after` has no front matter), the switch to the source carrying the edit over, a file Rust cannot patch opening in the source and its rich view read only, a conflict switching to the source, Milkdown read only with the first Markdown file, the body without the front matter, the switch to the source and back (the typing of the source in the next cut), the view kept while the tab is switched, and no button and no cut for a file that is not Markdown.

## A-27 Git history in the Orca file pane

The file pane of the Orca screen (A-18) has two parts, the files and the history, chosen by two icon buttons in its top row (A-34 adds a third part, the language server, and its button after them). The history lists the commits of the worktree the pane shows, the newest first, and under them the files the chosen commit changed; a double press on one of those files opens its diff as the tab of A-24 (the parent on the left, the commit on the right). Rust is `src-tauri/src/orca_files.rs` (the commands `orca_git_history`, `orca_commit_files` and `orca_commit_file_diff`); the frontend is `ts/orca-history.ts` (the list and the files), `ts/orca-files.ts` (the buttons, the heading and the read at the return of the focus), `ts/orca-diff.ts` (the tab) and `ts/orca.ts` (which worktree the pane shows, and the tab among the tabs).

Why. The pane showed the files as they are now and the diff from HEAD, so what a commit changed had to be looked up in another tool. The owner asked to see the history in the right column, as Orca's own window does (2026-11-13, issue #535; the parts #536 and #537 are Rust and #538 is the screen). The layout was chosen by the owner: layout A, the buttons in the top row of the pane, the commits above and the files of the chosen commit below.

The buttons. The top row of the pane (A-18) has, at its left, 「ファイル」 (a file icon) and 「履歴」 (the icon of a git branch, as Orca's own is), both `.icon-btn` with `aria-pressed` (true on the one that is shown), before the button 「ファイル欄」 at the right end. The files are shown at the start of the app. The choice is kept in memory only, for all worktrees, and is not lost when the pane is hidden and shown again. The part that is not chosen is hidden (`hidden`); the heading (A-18, with the branch button of A-25) is the same for both, and so is the read at the return of the focus, below (it reads the files when they are shown, the history when it is shown). The tree of the files is read only while the files are shown (the root of a worktree that was read is not read again by switching), and the history only while it is shown.

The list. It is under the heading, in the upper part of the pane (`#orca-history-list`), and has a row (`.orca-commit`, a button with `data-id` the full id of the commit) for each commit, in the order Rust gives, which is from HEAD, the newest first. A row has the subject (cut at the end of the line when long), under it the short id and the time (`formatDate`: the clock for today, the date for the others), and the author's name as its `title`. The chosen row has `aria-pressed` true. Pressing a row chooses its commit. A list with no commit says 「コミットがありません」.
- The first read takes 50 commits (`orca_git_history(path, limit 50, skip 0, environment)`), when the history is shown with a worktree. The commits that follow are read when the list is scrolled to its end (within 80 px of it), or when the 50 do not fill the list so that it cannot scroll, with `skip` the number of commits read so far, `limit` 50 and `historyToken` the token that the first answer gave (`orca_git_history(path, limit, skip, historyToken, environment)`; `historyToken` is null in the first read), while the answer says `hasMore`; they are added under the rows, and the rows already there are not drawn again (the scroll and the focus of the list stay), nor are they by a press on a commit or on a file. The token keeps those reads on the history the first page began, so a HEAD that moves meanwhile gives neither a commit twice nor a gap; a commit with an id already in the list is not added again in any case. When the answer says `historyChanged` (Orca no longer holds that history, for instance after a long idle time), the list is read again from the start. A read is not started while another is on its way. An answer with no commit ends the list, whatever `hasMore` says. A read that fails shows its message (in the warning color) under the rows and starts no read by scrolling; the next return of the focus reads again.
- The list belongs to one worktree of one target (the worktree of A-18's heading): another worktree gives a new list, read at once when the history is shown, and nothing is kept of the old one, the chosen commit included (an answer that comes late for the old one is dropped). When the window gets the focus back (as for the files in A-18, with the history shown and a worktree shown, the pane shown on the Orca screen, and no read of the list on its way), the list is read again from the start (`skip` 0) and the chosen commit is dropped, so that new commits show; with the pane hidden or the files shown nothing is read (the files are read, A-18). After a switch of the branch (A-25) the list is read again from the start, as the tree is. By the clock of A-18 (every 10 seconds while the history is shown), the first 50 commits are asked for again from HEAD as it is then (`skip` 0, no token): when they are the ones the list begins with, nothing is drawn (the scroll and the chosen commit stay); else they are the new list, and a chosen commit that it still holds stays chosen with its files. A read by the clock that fails says nothing.

The files of a commit. They are in the lower part (`#orca-history-files`), and appear when a commit is chosen. Pressing a commit reads `orca_commit_files(path, commitId, environment)` with the full id of the commit; its answer (the files, how many the commit changed, and the first parent) is drawn as a row (`.orca-hfile`, a button with `data-path` and `data-status`) for each file, in Rust's order: a mark of how it changed (M modified, A added, D deleted, R renamed, C copied; `data-status` is Orca's word), the file's name, the folder of it from the worktree in a faint color, and the numbers of lines added (+n) and removed (−n), none for a binary file. The `title` of a row is the path, and `old → new` for a renamed or copied file. A commit that changed more files than Rust lists (5,000) shows 「ほか {n} 件」 under the last one; a commit with no file says 「変更されたファイルはありません」. While the files are read there is a spinner, and a read that fails shows its message in place of the rows (in the warning color), as the list does. The answer for a commit that is no longer the chosen one, or for a list that is gone, is dropped. A single press on a file row only chooses it (`aria-pressed`).

The diff. A double press on a file row (or Enter on the focused row) opens its diff as a tab of the work pane (A-24) by `orca_commit_file_diff(path, commitId, parentId, relative, oldRelative, environment)`, with the full id of the commit, its first parent (`parentId` null for the first commit, which is compared with nothing), the path of the file from the worktree, and `oldRelative` the path the file had at the parent for a renamed or copied file (else null). The tab is the one of A-24 in every respect (the views, the colors, the buttons of the changes, the reasons, its id `diff:{n}`, its close), with these differences: the left side is the file at the parent and the right side the file at the commit; the heading has the short id of the commit after the path (`.orca-diff-commit`) and the title of the tab is 「{name} {short id}」; the tab is for one file of one commit, so a diff that is open already for the same worktree, path and commit is selected and read again (no second tab), and the same file of another commit, or of the working tree, is another tab; and 「読み直す」 repeats the same call. A file that the commit added is compared with nothing on the left and one that it deleted with nothing on the right. The reasons of A-24 (binary, too large, not UTF-8) are shown in place of the rows in the same way.
- The worktree is the one the file pane shows (A-18), so the tab opens in the project and the pane that A-24's does.

The calls. The three commands check the worktree against the target's list as `orca_file_read` does (A-18: believed for 60 seconds, an SSH host's worktree refused, a failed call makes the next one confirm it again), and check the arguments (`limit` 1 to 200; `historyToken`, when given, is at most 100 letters, digits and hyphens; a commit id and a parent id are full object ids; the paths are inside the worktree). They call `git.history`, `git.commitCompare` and `git.commitDiff` of Orca's runtime, over this machine's unix socket or a paired server's WebSocket (the connection of A-10). `orca_commit_file_diff` answers what `orca_file_diff` answers (A-24). Nothing here writes to the worktree. No subject, author, path or text of a commit is in a log or a file of the app, and `historyToken` is only Orca's handle of a list, held in memory, not a credential.

Checked against. `git.history`, `git.commitCompare` and `git.commitDiff` are not a public interface of Orca. The three were called on this machine's Orca 1.4.258 over its unix socket, and the shape of each answer was checked there (`requirements/constraints.md`, Orca); a saved environment (WebSocket) was not tried, and an Orca update can change them. `git.commitDiff` can answer an Orca-side git failure with empty texts and no error, so a diff that is shown empty may be a commit Orca could not read.

Unit tests (`cargo test`) cover the arguments and the answers of the three calls (`orca_files.rs`: the page of commits, `hasMore`, the token and `historyChanged`, a commit with no full id, the files of a commit and the limit, the not ready summary, the arguments of `git.commitDiff`, a file added or deleted by the commit). The E2E spec `orca-files.e2e.ts` mocks the commands and covers the two buttons and their state, the part shown, that the history is not read before it is shown and the files are not read again by switching back, the rows of the list (subject, short id, time), the next read at the end of the list with `skip` and the token and its stop at the last page, the rows kept by a press on a commit or a file, a commit given twice drawn once, the list read again from the start on `historyChanged`, the files of a commit (the call, the marks, names, folders and counts, the title of a renamed file, 「ほか {n} 件」), the answer dropped for a commit no longer chosen, the double press and Enter (the arguments of the call, with the parent null for the first commit and the old path of a renamed file, the tab, its title and the short id in its heading), the same file of the same commit selected and read again and another commit opening another tab, 「読み直す」 repeating the call of the commit, the reasons (of the diff, of the list, of the files; no commit; no file), the list read again from the start at the return of the focus (and not while the pane is hidden), the worktree of another tab giving a new list while the choice stays, a switch of the branch from the heading reading the list again, and the pane hidden and shown again.

## A-29 Scratchpad in the Orca work pane

The plus of the work pane (A-16) has a third item, 「スクラッチパッド」, that makes a file in `.orca/scratchpad/` of a worktree and opens it in the editor (A-18) as a tab of the pane with the focus. Rust is `orca_scratchpad_create` in `src-tauri/src/orca_files.rs`; the frontend is `ts/orca-scratchpad.ts` (the popover), `ts/orca.ts` (the menu and the worktree) and `ts/orca-files.ts` (the read again of the file pane).

Why. The owner asked for a place to jot down text next to the sessions, a file that stays with the worktree (2026-11-14, issue #582); the owner chose layout A of the two put to them: a small field under the plus that shows the folder, and the file opens at once. Orca 1.4.258 has no scratchpad of its own (`.orca/drops` and `.orca/shots` are its folders for pasted files and shots), so this is a plain file made with `files.createFile`.

The item. 「スクラッチパッド」 is after 「ターミナルを開く」, with a line icon (`SCRATCHPAD_SVG`), and is enabled and disabled as A-16 says. Choosing it picks the worktree as the other two items do (A-16, "Where": one worktree of the selected project acts at once; none or several open the dialog of A-6 narrowed to the project), and then opens the popover under the plus.

The popover. A `.menu` window (`.orca-scratch-pop`, `role="dialog"`, `aria-label` 「スクラッチパッド」) under the plus, with one row, a field (`.orca-scratch-field`), and a row of two buttons. The field shows the fixed text `.orca/scratchpad/` (not editable) and, after it, a text input (`aria-label` 「ファイル名」, no placeholder) that starts as `memo.md` with `memo` selected (the text before the last `.`) and has the keys. No other text explains it (`frontend.md`). The buttons are 「やめる」 and 「作って開く」 (`.text-btn.primary`). Enter in the input, or 「作って開く」, makes the file (a conversion of the IME is not an Enter); Escape, 「やめる」 and a press outside close it and make nothing. A blank name (empty or only spaces) does nothing and the popover stays. While a make is on its way the button is disabled and Enter does nothing.

Making it. The popover calls `orca_scratchpad_create(path, name, environment)` with the worktree and its place (the `environment` of the worktree's row). Rust checks the name as A-18 checks the name of a new file (`check_new_name`: one name, trimmed, not empty, `.` or `..`, no `/`, `\` or control character, at most 255 bytes; otherwise 「名前が正しくありません」 and Orca is not called), makes `.orca/scratchpad/{name}` with `files.createFile` (which makes the missing `.orca` and `.orca/scratchpad`, and never overwrites), and answers `{relative, existed}`: `relative` is the path from the worktree and `existed` is true when the name was taken (an answer of `EEXIST` or "already exists"), which is no failure: the file that is there is left as it is and is the one that opens. Any other failure is an error with Orca's message. It is a write, so the target's list is read each time (as a save does), an SSH host's worktree is refused, and nothing of the text is made, kept or logged by the app: the file is empty.

Opening. When the answer comes, the popover closes and the file opens in the editor as a tab of the focused pane and is selected (`openFile` of A-18, as a double press on a file of the tree does, also while the file pane is hidden), in the worktree that was chosen even when the file pane shows another. A failure shows its message on a card (A-28) and the popover stays open with the text, so that it can be pressed again. A selection of another project, or of another target, made while the make was on its way leaves the file made and not opened. When the file pane shows that worktree, the folders of the way to the file that the pane has read (the root, `.orca` and `.orca/scratchpad`, in that order) are read again, so that a folder that was not there, and the new file, show.

The call. It was checked in the `app.asar` of Orca 1.4.258 only for `files.createFile` (A-18, issue #584) and never run on a real Orca, and an Orca update can change it.

Unit tests (`cargo test`) cover the name and the path made of it, the call (`files.createFile` with `.orca/scratchpad/{trimmed name}` and the host), a taken name answered as the existing file, another failure left as an error, and a bad name that calls nothing. The E2E spec `orca.e2e.ts` covers the third item and its icon, its being enabled for a project's row, the popover (its place under the plus, the fixed folder, the name with `memo` selected, the keys, no placeholder), Enter and 「作って開く」 with the arguments of the call and the target, the opening of the editor, a taken name opening the existing file with no error, the three ways of closing, a blank name, a failure that keeps the popover, the dialog of the worktrees when the project has several, and the read again of the file pane.

## A-31 Open an Orca HTML file in the browser

An HTML file in the file pane of the Orca screen (A-18) can be opened in the default browser of this PC: the app serves the files of the worktree from an HTTP server of its own on `127.0.0.1`, and hands the address of the file to the system. Rust is `src-tauri/src/orca_preview.rs` (the command `orca_file_open_in_browser`, and the server); the frontend is `ts/orca-files.ts`.

Why. An HTML file of a worktree (a report, a mock, a built page) could be read in the editor only as its source, and a file of a saved environment (a paired server) is not on this PC, so the browser could not be pointed at it. The owner asked to see such a file as a page (issue #620, parts #621 and #622).

The menu. 「ブラウザで開く」 is the fifth item of the menu of a file row (A-18), after 「差分を表示」 and before the line, with a line icon at its left. Only a file whose name ends with `.html`, whatever the case, has it: the menu of any other file and of a folder (one named like an HTML file too) does not. A pick closes the menu and calls the command below. Nothing is said when it works (the browser comes to the front), and a failure shows its message on a card (A-28).

The command. `orca_file_open_in_browser(path, relative, environment)` takes the path of the worktree, the path of the file from it and the place of the worktree (the saved environment, or null for this machine), as the other commands of A-18 do. It refuses a relative path as A-18 does, and a path whose name does not end with `.html`, whatever the case (「HTML のファイルだけをブラウザで開けます」). A path with a part that starts with `.` (`.a.html`, `.git/a.html`, `a/.b/c.html`) is refused with the same message, because the server answers such a path 404. It checks the worktree against the target's list read just now, as a download does (an SSH host has no file calls and is refused, as in A-18), starts the server when it does not run yet, and opens `http://127.0.0.1:{port}/{token}/{path}` with `tauri-plugin-opener`. It answers nothing: the address holds the token and is not logged or put in an error (a failed open is 「ブラウザで開けません」). A failure shows its message on a card (A-28).

The address for the preview frame. `orca_file_preview_url(path, relative, environment)` takes the same three values, makes the same checks as `orca_file_open_in_browser` (the path, the `.html` name, the worktree against the target's list read just now, the SSH host refused) and starts the server in the same way, and returns the address `http://127.0.0.1:{port}/{token}/{path}` instead of opening it. It is the one place the address goes to the page of the app, and only for the frame of the HTML preview in the editor (issue #977; the frame is the part of issue #979): the page does not keep it, and it is not logged or put in an error. The window lets this frame load by two settings. The Content-Security-Policy of `src-tauri/tauri.conf.json` has `http://127.0.0.1:*` in `frame-src`, and the navigation check of the window (`navigation_of` in `src-tauri/src/lib.rs`) lets `http://127.0.0.1:{port}` through when `{port}` is the port the server is running on now (`orca_preview::running_port`, none while the server has not started); any other port, any other host (`localhost`, `127.0.0.2`) and `https` on that port are handled as before (a link to the browser).

The server. It listens on `127.0.0.1` alone, on a port the OS gives, and starts at the first open; it then runs until the app ends. Each worktree (a target and a path) that was opened has a token of its own, 16 random bytes of the OS in hex, held in memory only, so an address of an earlier run of the app opens nothing. One connection carries one request and is closed after the answer (`Connection: close`); a request must come within 10 seconds and its first line and headers within 8 KiB.

What it answers, in this order:

- A request that is no HTTP/1 request is 400.
- A `Host` header that is not exactly `127.0.0.1:{port}` (none, two, `localhost`, another name or port) is 403, before anything else is looked at: a page that reached the port through a name of its own (DNS rebinding) sends that name.
- A method other than GET and HEAD is 405, with `Allow: GET, HEAD`. The body of a request is never read.
- The target is `/{token}/{path}`, without what follows a `?` or a `#`. A token that no worktree has is 404. The path has its percent escapes taken out and is then held to the path check of A-18 (no empty path, no control character, no `.`, `..` or empty part, so an escaped `..` or `/` is seen as what it is and a path that ends with `/` is refused); a path that fails it is 404, and Orca is not asked. A path with a part that starts with `.` (`.env`, `.git/config`, `.npmrc`, `a/.b/c`), looked at after the escapes are taken out, is 404 too, and Orca is not asked: the script of an opened page could otherwise fetch the settings and the secrets that a worktree keeps under such names. A folder has no list.
- Otherwise the worktree is confirmed as a read of A-18 confirms it (the target's list, believed for 60 seconds): a worktree that left the list, and an SSH host, are 404. Then the file is read over a connection to Orca of its own, as the download of A-22 reads it (`files.stat` for the size, `files.readChunk` 384 KiB at a time and never past the size, or `files.readPreview` on a host that lacks either), and each chunk goes to the browser as it is read; nothing of the file is kept. A file that is not there is 404, and any other failure before the first byte is 502. A read that fails after the headers went out closes the connection short of the `Content-Length` it promised. A HEAD gets the same headers and no body, from the size `files.stat` gives: nothing of the file is read (only a host that lacks `files.stat` has the file read for its size, and the read stops once the size is known). After a failed connection to Orca or a 502 the confirmation of the worktree is dropped, so the next request asks the target's list again; a 404 for a file that is not there keeps it.

What is left open. No CSP `sandbox` is set on a page, because a `type="module"` script and the page's own `fetch` would stop working and the page would not show as it is. So the script of an opened page can read every file of the same worktree that has no part starting with `.`. Whether Orca's `files.stat` and `files.readChunk` follow a symbolic link inside the worktree (one named without a `.` that points at a file outside it, or at a hidden one) was not checked.

Any file of a worktree that has a token is answered, not only an HTML one, so that a page gets the images, styles and scripts it names by a relative path.

The headers. The `Content-Type` is decided from the extension of the name, whatever the case (`html` and `htm`, `css`, `js` and `mjs`, `json` and `map`, `txt` and `md`, `xml`, `svg`, `png`, `jpg` and `jpeg`, `gif`, `webp`, `avif`, `ico`, `pdf`, `wasm`, `woff`, `woff2`, `ttf`, `otf`, `mp4`, `webm`, `mp3`, `wav`; the text kinds as UTF-8), and any other name is `application/octet-stream`. Every answer, a refusal too, carries `Cache-Control: no-store`, `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` and `Referrer-Policy: no-referrer`; a refusal has no body.

The same command works on this machine's worktree and on a worktree of a saved environment. The bytes of a file, a path and a token are in no log and no file of the app.

Checked against. The calls are those of A-22 and were not tried again here. The server was run only against a fake Orca in the unit tests: no real browser was pointed at it, and a file of a real Orca was never served.

Unit tests (`cargo test`) cover the reading of a request (the first line, the `Host` header, two of them, what is no request), the `Host` check, the percent escapes taken out and put in, the token and the path of a target with the paths refused, the `Content-Type` of each extension, the order of the refusals, the token a worktree keeps, the random token, the paths the command opens, the address, the navigation check of the window (the running port of the server is allowed, and no other port, host or scheme, and nothing on `127.0.0.1` while the server has not started), and, over a local `TcpListener` with a fake Orca, a file served with its headers, a file beside the page by its escaped path, an empty file, a file of several chunks, a HEAD, a wrong token, a path out of the worktree, a method other than GET and HEAD, a `Host` that is not the server's, what is no request and a request over 8 KiB, a file that is not there, a read that fails before the first byte and one that fails midway, and an Orca without `files.readChunk`. E2E (`e2e/specs/orca-files.e2e.ts`) covers the item, fifth in the menu of an HTML file and absent from another file and from a folder, the command called with the worktree, the path and the target, with the menu closed and nothing shown, and a failure shown on a card (A-28).

## A-33 This machine and a saved environment in one list

While a saved environment is the target (A-6), the list of the Orca screen (A-8) holds this machine's items and the saved environment's in one: the projects and the sessions of both, with the name of the saved environment on the rows that are its. When this machine is the target nothing changes: one place, no chip.

Why. The owner chose, for the work to connect to this machine and a saved environment at once (#133, option B), one list with the name of the environment on its rows over two lists side by side (2026-11, issue #337). At most one saved environment is connected at a time, the one chosen as the target (A-6).

The list. `orca_overview` answers the target's lists and, beside them, this machine's as `local` (A-6, "Where an item is"). The screen draws the two as one: this machine's projects first, then the target's, with the projects sorted by `atMs`, newest first, as each list is (a stable sort, so this machine's comes first on a tie). The projects with a session at work still come first, in the order they began (A-8), and the sessions of both places are drawn in the list as A-8 says. A project's key is its repository's id, which is not the same in two places, so the rows do not meet. A session row, a tab, a terminal, a worktree of the plus (A-16), the files and the layout of A-17 take their place from the item they belong to ("Where an operation goes", A-6); the terminals of this machine are mirrored and those of the saved environment are streamed, in one list as in two. The mix has no pure function of Rust: the target's list arrives by the stream (A-12) and this machine's by a read of its own, apart, so the screen holds each and draws both (`remix` in `ts/orca.ts`).

The chip. A project row (A-8) of the saved environment end the name with a chip (`.orca-env`) that holds the environment's name, as text (`textContent`), and its `title` is the name (the name is cut at 9em). A row of this machine has none. A session row (A-8) has none, saved environment or not: the project row above it says which environment it is in (at the owner's instruction, 2026-11-16; issue #711). The tabs of the work pane, the terminals and the dialog of the plus (A-16) show no place. The chip is what `environment` (A-6) says of the item, so an item that has none is this machine's.

The reads. This machine's list is read with the target's by `orca_overview` (A-6) when the screen is shown, after a failure and in a poll of a target that is not streamed. While the target is streamed (A-12) the clock does not read it, and this machine's list beside it is read every 10 seconds while the screen is shown (`orca_destination` with `environment` null and `force` false, the read of the pane of A-9; no new command), and nothing is read by the clock while the screen is hidden. After a start or a close in a project of this machine (A-8, A-16) the list of this machine is read at once, even if a read of it is on its way (the older one's return is dropped), and a session started there is followed as A-6 says, also while its last read failed (the wait ends after 30 seconds). A read of `orca_overview` that is on its way puts off the clock's read of this machine by one period.

When a read fails. The two reads fail apart, and the list keeps drawing the other's items. A read of the target that failed leaves its last good rows while the target is the same, as A-6 says, or none before the first good one, and this machine's items stay. A read of this machine that failed keeps the last good items of this machine (none before the first good one) and the target's stay.

The notice. The notice above the list (`#orca-notice`, A-6) says each read that failed, as a line of its own in the failed style (`.pending.fail`), with the name of the place and the reason: 「このマシン を読み込めませんでした: {message}」 for this machine, then 「{name} を読み込めませんでした: {message}」 for the saved environment that is the target (this machine's first, as in the list). When one read fails the line is only its place's and the other place's rows stay drawn; when both fail both lines show; a line goes when its place is read well again (the next read of `orca_overview`, the read of this machine by the clock, or a list of the stream). A saved environment that is the target has its name in the line also when only it fails and this machine is read well, and when this machine is the target the line is the one of A-6, with no name. A missing `orca` command is said as A-6 says, once and with no name (it is one command for both places, so this machine's read says nothing of it). The pane of A-9 and the terminal pane of the database screen say a read as before (`noticeOf`). Only one saved environment is connected at a time, so there are at most two lines (#338; the status of the two side by side, in the footer, was dropped at the owner's instruction, 2026-11-16).

The count and the notices. The count of the Orca button, the Dock badge, the notices of the stopped agents and the mark of the working agents (A-6) are the target's alone: the sessions of this machine that wait or stop are not counted while a saved environment is the target.

Not here. A second saved environment connected at the same time, a status of each place beside the list (the footer, dropped), and a place shown on a tab, in a terminal or in the dialog of the plus are not offered. Two items of the two places with one key (one project key) are not told apart, and this machine's comes first.

No new command, no new saved file, no new call to an outside service.

Unit tests (`cargo test`): none new; the lists, the place of each item and `local` are those of A-6. E2E (`orca.e2e.ts`, "orca: this machine and a saved environment in one list") covers the projects of both in one list in the order of their activity with the chip on the rows of the saved environment only, no chip on the session rows of either place and on no tab, no chip when this machine is the target, a session of this machine that waits not counted by the button, a start in a project of this machine that is followed through the read of this machine, the read of this machine by the clock while the target is streamed with no read of the target, a failed read of this machine and a lost connection of the target each leaving the other's projects and each said by a line of the notice that names its place, both failing at once with both lines, the line going when its place is read well, the first read of either failing, and the projects of this machine going when the target is changed to it.

## A-34 Go to a definition in the Orca editor

The editor of A-18 goes to the definition of a name with a language server, ruby-lsp for Ruby (#696). A project has at most one language server for each language of A-21, and the language is the server's key in every command (#957 and #973; the Rust side is #958 and #974, which is the part of this section that says so below, and #975 is the cards of the screen; the ssh select is #959, the definition tab #962, the cards of the settings #961 and the underline of the Command key #963: the screen shows and edits every server of a repository, and asks for a definition of any file whose language a server takes). This section holds what the Rust side decides, saves and runs (issues #697, #698 and #699, `src-tauri/src/lsp.rs`, `lsp/protocol.rs`, `lsp/server.rs` and `lsp/outside.rs`) and the screen of the definition (issues #700 and #962, `lsp/view.rs`, `ts/orca-definition.ts`, `ts/orca-editor.ts` and `ts/orca.ts`); the screen of the server's state and settings, its start and stop, and the start of the container (issue #701, `lsp/container.rs`, `ts/orca-lsp.ts`) is the last part of the same requirement and is below.

Why. The owner works in Ruby projects that run in Docker, and on the machines of saved environments (A-33), so the server cannot be assumed to be on the host (2026-11-15, issue #696). The way to start it is decided from the worktree, so that the owner sets nothing in the usual case, and the settings of a repository can replace what was decided.

How the server is started, in this order (`decide`, a pure function of two texts):

1. A compose file. When the worktree's folder holds `compose.yaml`, `compose.yml`, `docker-compose.yaml` or `docker-compose.yml`, `docker compose config --format json` is run in it, and the services whose `volumes` hold a bind (`type` `bind`) of the worktree itself (`source` equal to its path, a `/` at the end of either left off) with an absolute `target` are the candidates, in the order of their names. That output has the anchors and extensions expanded and every volume in the long form, so a service that took its volumes from an anchor is listed as any other. A named volume, a tmpfs, a bind of another folder (a folder inside the worktree, or its parent), and a service whose name is empty or starts with `-` are not candidates; a service that mounts the worktree twice counts once, at its first mount. The command is `docker compose exec -T -w {target} {service} {command}` (`bundle exec ruby-lsp` for ruby, the command of the table below for the other languages), and the path of the worktree where it runs is the `target`. `-w` is needed because `exec` runs in the working folder of the image or of the service, which is not always the mount (the `ruby:3.3` image works in `/`, where Bundler finds no `Gemfile` and ruby-lsp ends with "Could not locate Gemfile or .bundle/ directory"). The `target` and the service are quoted for a POSIX shell. All the candidates are returned and the first is used unless the settings choose another. Whether a container is running is not asked here.
2. The host. For ruby, a `Gemfile.lock` that lists the gem `ruby-lsp` (a spec or a dependency line; `ruby-lsp-rails` and a URL that holds the name are not it): `bundle exec ruby-lsp` on the host, and the worktree's own path is the path. For any other language the host is always the place, and the command is looked for there (below).
3. Neither (ruby with no such lock): the server is not used.

A compose file whose services do not mount the worktree leaves the decision to the `Gemfile.lock`. A compose configuration that cannot be read as JSON is an error and is not passed over.

The command, when the settings have none (#974). It is the first server of the table below that exists in the place decided (the service of compose that was chosen, else the host); a language that the table lacks, and a place not decided, have none (`LspLaunch.command` is none), so the settings need a `command`. Ruby's is `bundle exec ruby-lsp`, which nothing is asked for. For any other language one script is run in that place, in the worktree: on the host by the route of the project, in a service by `docker compose exec -T -w {mount} {service} sh -c {script}`. It prints a line `have {command}` for each command of the table that exists (`command -v` for a program of the PATH, `[ -x node_modules/.bin/... ]` for one of the project), and nothing of the worktree is read but that. A container that does not run, and a Docker daemon that cannot be reached (the last line of the standard error says `Cannot connect to the Docker daemon` or `Is the docker daemon running?`), cannot be asked, so the first command of the table is taken and the start says that the container is down (the state `containerDown`); any other failure of the script is an error of the start. `lsp_launches` runs the script once for each place, whatever the number of servers, and a place that fails in that other way leaves the `command` of the servers that run in it out (`LspLaunch.command` is none: the card shows no way) and does not fail the read for the others; the start of such a server says the failure. The services that mount the worktree and the mount are decided for every server alike, and the `Gemfile.lock` is read only for ruby.

The table (`lsp/detect.rs`, the owner's decision of 2026-11-18, #973). A language is in the worktree when one of its files is there:

| Language | Marks | Servers, in order (name: command) |
|----------|-------|-----------------------------------|
| ruby | a `Gemfile.lock` that lists `ruby-lsp` | ruby-lsp: `bundle exec ruby-lsp` |
| rust | `Cargo.toml` | rust-analyzer: `rust-analyzer` |
| typescript, javascript (one server: the table gives both the same, and the server of TypeScript takes the files of JavaScript too) | `package.json`, `tsconfig.json` | typescript-language-server: `node_modules/.bin/typescript-language-server --stdio`, else `typescript-language-server --stdio` |
| go | `go.mod` | gopls: `gopls` |
| python | `pyproject.toml`, `requirements.txt` | pyright: `pyright-langserver --stdio`, else pylsp: `pylsp` |

Finding. `lsp_detect(environment, project, worktree)` answers a `LspFound` for each language of the table that the worktree has, in the order of the table, and none for a language it does not show: `language`, `name` (the name of the server found, else of the first server of the table), `command` (what is run in the worktree, with the `docker compose exec -T -w {mount} {service}` in front when it runs in a service; none unless found), `place` (`{kind: "host"}` or `{kind: "compose", service}`), `mount` (the path the worktree has there; its own path on the host) and `state`: `found`, `missing` (the language is there and no server of the table is) or `containerDown` (the service does not run, so nothing was asked). It checks the worktree (below) and reads it by the route of the project, as `lsp_launches` does: the compose configuration, then one script on the host that prints `mark {file}` for each mark file that is there, `lock {line}` for each line of `Gemfile.lock` that holds `ruby-lsp` and `have {command}` for each command of the table, and, when a service mounts the worktree and a language of the worktree has a command to look for (ruby's is not looked for), the same script without the marks in the first service (the files are the worktree's, which the service mounts, so the marks are read once). The places and the commands are decided by pure functions of what was printed (`parse_probe`, `found_from`). It saves nothing and starts nothing.

Where it is read. A worktree of this machine is read on the spot, by `/bin/sh -c`. A worktree of a saved environment (A-33) is read through this Mac's `ssh` to the host in the settings of its repository (`sshHost`, a Host of `~/.ssh/config`, refused when it is empty, starts with `-` or holds whitespace or a control character): Orca's environments say nothing of ssh, so the owner names it, by choosing it from the Hosts of this Mac's `~/.ssh/config` (`ssh_hosts`, the fields below). `docker compose config` is run only when the folder has a compose file, and its failure (no `docker`, a broken file) is an error with ssh's or the shell's last line; the `Gemfile.lock` is read with `cat`, and only for ruby and only when no service of the compose configuration mounts the worktree (the lock then decides nothing). Both are read up to 4 MiB, within 30 seconds, as UTF-8, and nothing of them is kept. A project of an SSH host of Orca (its key is `ssh:{host}`) has no worktree to read and is refused.
The same route starts the server (`lsp_start`, below): the script is `PATH` with `/usr/local/bin` and `/opt/homebrew/bin` added (a program that is not started from a login shell does not have the folders of `docker`), `cd` to the worktree and the command; on this machine it is the argument of `/bin/sh -c`, and by ssh it is `sh -c '{the script, quoted}'` as the one element after the host, so that the login shell of the host reads it as it is. The worktree path and the service name are quoted; the command of the settings is the owner's own text and is run as written.

The settings of a repository are in `lsp-settings.json` in the app config directory (`{"version": 3, "repositories": [...]}`), one entry for each pair of the place of the project (`environment`: `null` for this machine, else the name of the saved environment) and its key (`OrcaProject.key`), which is the repository id of Orca. An entry holds `sshHost` and `servers`, the language servers of the repository in order. A server holds `language` (its key: the language of A-21 it takes, one), `name` (what the screen shows; an empty one is saved as the name of the table for the language, else as the language), `autoStart` (true when nothing is saved), `command` and `mount` (replace the command that is run in the worktree and the path the worktree has where it runs; none means what was decided) and `service` (the choice among the candidates; a name that is not one is ignored). A repository with nothing saved has one server, `ruby-lsp`, of the language `ruby`, which starts by itself, with nothing replaced (the behavior before #958). With a `command` and no method decided, the path is the worktree's own unless `mount` says otherwise. An entry that is the same as that is not kept. The name of a server is trimmed, has no control character and has at most 64 characters (two servers may have one name); `language` is a language that `detect_language` of A-21 can answer (`orca_language::is_language`, `plaintext` included), and no language is taken by two servers of a repository, so that a language has one server (`server_for`; the screen asks it through `lsp_server_for(environment, project, language)`, which answers the language, which is the key, or none when the repository has no server of it; JavaScript with no server of its own gets the server of TypeScript, `server_for`, #976). A refusal is a message in Japanese. A repository may be saved with no server. A text is trimmed, and an empty one is not set; a text over 4096 characters or with a control character, a `mount` that is not an absolute path, and an `sshHost` that is empty, starts with `-` or holds whitespace are refused. A file of version 2 (a server with `languages`, a list) is read as one server of each language with the name and the settings of that server, in the order of its languages; a file of version 1 (an entry of `autoStart`, `command`, `mount`, `service` and `sshHost`, which was one ruby-lsp) is read as an entry with `sshHost` and the one server `ruby-lsp` of `ruby` with those settings; nothing is written by a read, and the next save writes every entry as version 3. A file that cannot be read, or has another `version`, is an error that names it, and a save never replaces it.

Commands. `lsp_settings(environment, project)` answers the saved settings, or the unset ones. `lsp_settings_set(settings)` saves them and answers what was saved. `lsp_language_looks()` answers how a card draws each language of A-21 (the title and the icon, each language once, sorted, `plaintext` too; the screen part below). `lsp_launches(environment, project, worktree, servers)` (`servers`: the languages of servers of the repository, which are their keys; one it has no server of is an error before anything is run, and so is a text that is not a language of A-21) first checks the worktree (below), then reads it once for all the servers (the compose configuration once, the `Gemfile.lock` once when the server of ruby is among them, and only that server sees the lock, and the script of the table once for each place that a server with no command runs in) and answers a `LspLaunch` for each language, in the order of `servers`: `method` (`compose`, `host` or `none`), `candidates` (service and mount), `service` (the one used), `command` and `mount`, with the settings applied. `lsp_detect` is described above. The types are ts-rs's (`LspLaunch`, `LspCandidate`, `LspMethod`, `LspRepoSettings`, `LspFound`, `LspFoundPlace`, `LspFoundState`, `LspLanguageLook` in `ts/bindings/`).
The worktree is checked. `lsp_detect`, `lsp_launches`, `lsp_start`, `lsp_container_start` and `lsp_read_outside`, which run a command in a worktree, take its path and its project from the screen, so each first asks Orca's list of that place, as the other Orca commands do (`orca_files::check_worktree_of_project`): a path that the list does not have, and a project that is not the worktree's own, are refused with no command run. `lsp_stop`, `lsp_status`, `lsp_definition` and `lsp_definition_exists` only look up a server that is there, and need no such check.

The server (issue #698). `lsp_start(environment, project, worktree, server)` starts the server `server` the way `lsp_launches` reads it (the same script and route, `spawn_of`) as a child process of the app, and keeps it, one for each triple of the place of the project, the worktree and the language of the server (`server::Key`). It answers the state at once, `indexing`; the start is done by a task that reads the worktree, starts the process, and talks to it. A server that is there and not stopped is left as it is, and one that is stopped is started again (the screen's restart). A worktree path that is not absolute, or has a control character, is refused; every other failure (no way to start it, no ssh Host, a failed `docker compose config`, a process that cannot run) is the state `stopped` with its message. The talk is LSP over the process's standard input and output: `Content-Length` frames, `initialize` (no process id; the worktree as the server sees it, the `mount`, is the root; UTF-16 positions; progress is shown by the client, as ruby-lsp sends its indexing only then), `initialized`, the answers to the server's own requests (`window/workDoneProgress/create` and `client/registerCapability` get `null`, any other a "method not found" error), `textDocument/didOpen`, `textDocument/didChange`, `textDocument/definition`, `textDocument/didClose`, `workspace/didChangeWatchedFiles`, then `shutdown` and `exit`.
The state. `lsp_status(environment, worktree, server)` answers `LspStatus` (`state`, and for `stopped` the `reason` and a `message`), and each change of it is the event `lsp-status` (`LspStatusEvent`: `environment`, `worktree`, `server`, `status`; the screen listens once for all the worktrees and servers). It is `indexing` from the start until `initialized` and then until the end of every progress that ruby-lsp began (`$/progress`: `begin` opens a token, `end` closes it, at most 64 tokens), `running` after it, and `stopped` when the process is gone. A server that has shown no progress 3 seconds after `initialized` is taken as `running` (a progress that began holds `indexing` until it ends). `stopped` has the reason `notStarted` (there is no server for the worktree, or `lsp_stop` took it away), `containerDown` (the process ended and the last line of its standard error says that the container of the service is not running, `is not running` or `No container found`; an earlier line says nothing, so a server that ended for another reason is `failed`; the message is 「サービスのコンテナが動いていません」, so that the screen can offer the button that starts the container below; the app never starts it by itself) or `failed` (the message is what ended it, with the last line of the standard error or, for ssh's own failure, the failed connection). A server that ended is kept in that state until it is stopped or started again.
The definition. `lsp_definition(environment, worktree, server, relative, text, line, character)` first tells the server which files of its languages changed on the disk ("Files that change on the disk" below), then sends `text`, the editor's present text of the file `relative` (a path from the worktree: no empty name, `.` or `..`, no absolute path, no control character), as the whole text of the file (`didOpen`, with the `languageId` that `detect_language` of A-21 gives the file `relative` and `text`; more than 1 MiB is refused), asks `textDocument/definition` at the position (UTF-16 units, which is the index of a string of the editor, so nothing is converted), and answers the places as `LspLocation` (`path`, `outside`, `line`, `character`), at most 100, each once. The answer is read as `null` (none), a `Location`, or a list of `Location` and `LocationLink` (the place of the name, `targetSelectionRange`, else `targetRange`); an entry that cannot be read, or whose URI is not a `file:///` one, is left out. A path is decoded, its `.` and `..` are taken out, and it is mapped by the mount: below the mount it is `path` from the worktree (`outside` false); the mount itself is no file and is left out; any other path (a gem, for example) is the server's absolute path with `outside` true, and it is remembered for that server (`LspServers::outside_way`), because only such paths may be read from outside the worktree (#699). The answer is given to the screen first, then the file is closed (`textDocument/didClose`) unless another question about it still waits, so that a file is open for the server only while it is asked about: the server does not re-read an open file when it changes on the disk. The one exception is the question of the underline (`lsp_definition_exists`): it asks again and again about the same text, so its file stays open (one file at a time, with only the hash of its text kept) and the same text is not sent again; it is closed when the underline asks about another file, when a change of the files on the disk is told to the server, and by the next question of `lsp_definition` about it. The `languageId` of `didOpen` is the name of A-21 but for `shell` (`shellscript`) and the files `.tsx` and `.jsx` (`typescriptreact`, `javascriptreact`). A server that is started again after it stopped (the container) keeps the outside paths of the one it replaces, and so does the restart (`lsp_restart(environment, project, worktree, server)`, which has the same checks and the same start as `lsp_start`: it stops the server that is there, running or not, and starts the new one in its place, with the outside paths of the old one), because the list of definitions that shows them is still on the screen. `lsp_stop` followed by `lsp_start` would lose them, since a stop takes the server away with its paths. `lsp_definition_exists(environment, worktree, server, relative, text, line, character)` asks the same question for the underline ("The underline" below) and answers whether it has a place; it does not run the look at the disk, keeps no outside path, and refuses a server that is not `running` (`LspServers::definition_exists`, which shares the question with `definition`: `NOT_STARTED` for none, the stopped one's message for a stopped server and `NOT_READY` for one that is indexing). A server that is not running, a question before `initialized`, more than 32 questions waiting, an error of the server (its message) and no answer within 10 seconds are errors in Japanese.
Stopping. `lsp_stop(environment, worktree, server)` says `shutdown` and `exit` (each waited for 2 seconds at most), closes the standard input of the process, which ends the server (also through `docker compose exec` and ssh), and kills a process that is still there after 2 seconds; a worktree with no server is no error. The app does it for every server when it ends (`lib.rs`, `RunEvent::Exit`). When to stop (the tabs of the worktree are all closed, the worktree is gone from the list) is the screen's to decide ("The language server in the file pane" below).
Files that change on the disk. A server such as ruby-lsp indexes the worktree when it starts, registers no file watcher, and takes a change of a file on the disk only as the notice `workspace/didChangeWatchedFiles` from the client, so before each `lsp_definition` the app looks at the worktree and tells it (`lsp/watch.rs`, `LspServers::refresh`). One command is run in the worktree by the route and the way the server was started by (`run_on`): `find . {folders left out} -type f \( -name '*{extension}' -o … \) -mmin -{n} -print` (the extensions of the languages the server takes, from the tables of `detect_language`, `orca_language::extensions_of`, so `*.rb`, `*.rake` and the like for ruby-lsp; a server whose languages have no extension looks at nothing), the line `--`, the same `find` without `-mmin`, and the line `--end` when the second one ended well, as one script (`.git`, `node_modules`, `vendor/bundle`, `tmp`, `log` and `.claude/worktrees`, where the children of a formation have their worktrees (A-37), are left out; `-mmin`, `-path` and `-prune` are in GNU, BSD and BusyBox `find`; the host's files are the container's, because the worktree is a bind mount). `n` is the minutes since the last look, rounded up, plus one, and the first look counts from the start of the server. Nothing is written in the worktree, at most 4 MiB are kept, and it is given 5 seconds: a look that cannot be run, is cut or is late, or whose script did not run (no `--` line: the `cd` or the ssh failed), tells nothing and the question goes on without it. The exit code of the script does not decide: a folder that `find` cannot read makes the code 1, and what it listed is told all the same, while a full list without `--end` is not taken as all the files (so nothing is told as deleted, and the files known at the last look stay). The first look is made once when the server has answered `initialize` (at `initialized`) and tells nothing: it only sets the files known, so that a file that is gone or renamed between the start and the first question is told as deleted at that question. The files that changed are told as `changed` (2) and, when the full list says they were not there at the last look, as `created` (1); a file of the full list that was not known is `created` too, and a known file that is not in it any more is `deleted` (3), the last two only when the full list ended well (it ends with `--end`). The server is told at most 2000 files in one notice, each once. The paths of the last full list are kept in memory for that server (`LspServers`, nothing of a file's text).

A file outside the worktree (issue #699). `lsp_read_outside(environment, project, worktree, server, path)` answers `LspOutsideFile` (`path`, `text`, `language` and `highlight`, the name of A-21 that `detect_language` gives the path and the name of the highlighter, as `OrcaFileText` has them): the text, read only, of a file that the definition put outside the worktree (a gem). Only a path that the server of that (place, worktree, language) answered with `outside` true (`LspServers::outside_way`) is read; any other path, and a worktree with no server, is an error in Japanese before anything is run, so the app never reads an arbitrary path of the container or of the host. The path is the server's own (inside the container for compose): absolute, not the root, with no control character, no `.`, `..` or empty name between the slashes, and at most 4096 characters (`check_path`; the paths that `lsp_definition` gives pass it).
It is read the way the server was started: the route, the method and the service that the server's task decided when it started are kept with the server (`server::Way`), and the worktree is not read again, so a setting changed since the start, or a compose file changed, cannot send the read to another container or host than the server's own. The command, run in the worktree through the same `/bin/sh -c` or the ssh in front (`run_on`), is `docker compose exec -T {service} cat -- {path}` when the method is `compose` (the service used), and `cat -- {path}` when it is `host`; with no way decided (`none`), even when the settings have a `command`, there is no way to read and it is an error. The command is one run, and the worktree and its project are checked first ("The worktree is checked" above). The service and the path are quoted for a POSIX shell, and `--` ends the options of `cat`. At most 1 MiB is read (the run is asked for one byte more, and a longer file is an error), within 30 seconds; a file that is not UTF-8, a `cat` that fails (its last line is in the message, and ssh's own failure is told as a failed connection) and a container that does not run are errors, and nothing of the file is kept or written.

The definition tab (issue #700, and issue #962 of #957). The owner chose the layout (issue #696, option 1; for the definition, #957, option 1): the answer is a tab of the work pane under the pane of the editor (A-17), and the file pane has no list of definitions.
- The press. In the editor (A-18) of a file whose language a server of its project takes, in the source view (not the rich view of a Markdown file), the right button with the Command key (`contextmenu` with `metaKey`) on a character asks for its definition and leaves the default menu out. The server is the one `lsp_server_for(environment, project, language)` answers for the language Rust named for the file (A-21), asked once the text of the file is shown; a language that no server takes (none answered, or the answer not in yet) asks nothing and leaves the default menu, a server set after the file was read is found when the file is read again, and so is one that was taken away. The press does not ask for a file outside the worktree (below), and the right button without the Command key is as it was (the default menu, nothing asked). The place is the character under the mouse, not the caret: the layer of colors under the textarea carries the same text in the same place, so it is asked for the character at the point (`caretRangeFromPoint`; the textarea lets the point through for that moment), taking the one the caret is before or after that the point is on. A point on no character (past the end of a line, below the last line) asks nothing. `lsp_word(line, character)` gives the name there from the text of the line (Rust: letters, digits and `_`, with `@`, `@@` or `$` before them and one `?` or `!` after them, a `!` before `=` being the operator; none where there is no name, and then nothing is asked and the pane stays as it is). The question is `lsp_definition(environment, worktree, server, relative, text, line, character)`, where `server` is that server, with the editor's present text (typed and not saved included), the line from 0 and the character in UTF-16 units, which is the index of a string of the page.
- The underline (issue #963 of #957). While the Command key is down (`metaKey` of the `mousemove`, or the key `Meta` going down while the mouse is on the text), a mouse that stays on a character for 150 ms (the wait starts again at each move; a move inside the box of the name that is underlined changes nothing) asks whether the name has a definition: `lsp_word` gives the name from the character as it does for the press (the same place: `caretRangeFromPoint` on the layer of colors), and `lsp_definition_exists(environment, worktree, server, relative, text, line, character)` the answer, true when the server gives at least one place. It is the question of `lsp_definition` with the same text, line and character and the same limit of 10 seconds, but it tells the server of no change on the disk (`LspServers::refresh` is not run), keeps no place outside the worktree for `lsp_read_outside`, and is asked only of a server that is `running`, and the screen sends nothing to a server that it knows to be indexing or stopped (a server of which nothing is known is asked, and Rust says): one that is indexing, one that ended and none at all are errors in Japanese (`LspServers::definition_exists`), and nothing is started for the underline. A name with a definition gets an underline of 1px in the accent color (`.orca-edit-link`, `hidden` otherwise) in the box of the text (`.orca-edit-text`) under the layer of colors, as wide as the name and at the foot of its letters, whose place is read from the layer (the text of the layer sits where the text of the textarea does), and the cursor over the textarea is the pointer. A name with none, an error, and a name with no word draw nothing and say nothing; the Command key alone opens nothing, and the definition is opened by the right button as before. The answer is kept by the file and the range of the name (the line, and the start and the end in it) until the text of the file changes (a key, a change of the disk that replaces the text, the file read again), so a name is not asked again however often the key goes down and up or the mouse comes back to it; an error is not kept. The underline is taken away, with the wait and the answer that is on its way (an answer that comes after is dropped), when the key goes up, the window loses the focus, the mouse leaves the textarea or moves off the underlined name with no Command key or to another place, a key is typed, the text scrolls, the text is replaced by the disk's, and the tab is closed. Like the press, it asks nothing in the rich view of a Markdown file, for a language that no server takes and for a file outside the worktree.
- The tab. The press opens the definition tab of the project: a tab of the work pane (`definition:{n}`) with the target icon `DEFINITION_SVG` and the title 「定義 ・ {name}」, one for each project. Like the tab of a difference (A-24) it is kept in memory only, by target and project: it is not saved in a file (the saved layout may hold its id, which the next start mends away, A-17), and a tab that no pane shows keeps its rows and its scroll. When the project has none, it is made and split off below the pane of the editor that asked (the tab is moved to the bottom edge of that pane through `orca_panes`, A-17: a new pane under it, which has the focus, and the pane of the editor keeps its tab); when it is open already, in whatever pane, its content is replaced by the answer, it is selected and the layout stays. Its close button closes it, and a pane left with no tab closes as any pane does (A-17). It is a tab of the worktree the question was about, so the file pane shows that worktree while it is selected, and the server of the worktree is not stopped while it is open ("The stop" below). The press shows neither the file pane nor changes the part it shows.
- The content. The top of the tab (`.orca-definition-head`, as high as the heading of an editor) has the name, in the editor's font, and, when the answer is in, the count 「{n} 件」. While the answer is awaited the tab has the spinner. An answer that comes late for an older question, or for a tab that was closed, is dropped. A refusal or a failure of the server (no server, not ready, no answer in time) is drawn in the tab as Rust words it, with the name and no count. No place is 「定義はありません」 with 「0 件」.
- The rows. `lsp_places(locations)` gives, for each place in the order of the answer, how it is drawn (`LspPlace`: `file`, `line` from 1, `place`, `outside`, `language`: the name of A-21 that `detect_language` gives the path), a pure function (`place_of`). A row (`.orca-def`, with `data-index`) has the place (`.orca-def-names`, its `title` the path) and the button 「開く」 (`.orca-def-open`, a `.text-btn`). The first line of the place (`.orca-def-file`) is the path from the worktree and the line, 「app/lib/rails_ext.rb:7」, for a file in the worktree, and the file and the line, 「rails.rb:19」, for a file outside it; for a place outside the worktree `place` is under it (`.orca-def-place`) and the mark 「gem」 (`.orca-def-gem`) is before the button, for a Ruby file only (`language` is `ruby`). `place` is the folder from the worktree for a file in it (「.」 for its top), and for a file outside it the gem, its version and the folder in it (「railties 8.1.4.1 / lib」: the folder after the first `gems` folder that names an installed gem `{name}-{version}`, where the name may hold a `-` or a digit and the version may have a platform; or the gem that Bundler checked out of git, `bundler/gems/{name}-{commit}`, with the first 7 characters of the commit), else the folder as the server has it.
- The lines. Every row is open from the start, with no button to close it.
